People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I don’t want to be dramatic. But yeah, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound up after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I feel this kind of panic. Like I’m losing control, I guess. And then I’m already thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know everyone’s making a pretty big deal about it, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But I’m still jogging, I’m still covering my territory, I’m still hitting my numbers at work, so it doesn’t feel like I’m in some kind of medical emergency or anything. I guess I’d say it’s lower than people are comfortable with, but… I don’t know, I feel like I’m functioning okay.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s the only reason, or like I’m being irrational, but… yeah, a lot of it is that. If I have a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami and the scale is even a pound higher afterward, I get this sort of panic, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already planning how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess gaining weight is a pretty big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is the main thing that drives you to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow or dramatic, but if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already figuring out how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess that fear is a big driver.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat or overweight now, even though other people are telling you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I’m, like, fat exactly… I know that probably wouldn’t make sense to you if everyone’s saying I’m too thin. But when I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I kind of zoom in on my stomach or my face. If I look even a little soft, it really bothers me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult… it’s more like I worry other people are going to see that and think I look sloppy or weak. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d call it normal exactly. Like, I can understand on paper that maybe other people don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut when I’m looking in the mirror before a meeting or a date, I kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look even a little soft, it really bothers me. I start worrying people are going to notice and think I look sloppy, or weak. I know that might sound excessive… but that’s honestly where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant amount of weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess it’s been noticeable. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but HR and my sister have both been saying I’ve gotten too thin, which is kind of why I’m here.\n\nFrom my side, I mostly just noticed my suits fitting sharper, you know? But… yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s the same, honestly. And if I think I look soft at all, it’s hard for me not to assume other people will notice it too… like they’ll think I look sloppy or weak. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But I’m still jogging, I’m still covering my territory, I’m still hitting my numbers at work, so it doesn’t feel like I’m in some kind of medical emergency or anything. I guess I’d say it’s lower than people are comfortable with, but… I don’t know, I feel like I’m functioning okay.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s the only reason, or like I’m being irrational, but… yeah, a lot of it is that. If I have a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami and the scale is even a pound higher afterward, I get this sort of panic, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already planning how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess gaining weight is a pretty big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is the main thing that drives you to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow or dramatic, but if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already figuring out how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess that fear is a big driver.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat or overweight now, even though other people are telling you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I’m, like, fat exactly… I know that probably wouldn’t make sense to you if everyone’s saying I’m too thin. But when I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I kind of zoom in on my stomach or my face. If I look even a little soft, it really bothers me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult… it’s more like I worry other people are going to see that and think I look sloppy or weak. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d call it normal exactly. Like, I can understand on paper that maybe other people don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut when I’m looking in the mirror before a meeting or a date, I kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look even a little soft, it really bothers me. I start worrying people are going to notice and think I look sloppy, or weak. I know that might sound excessive… but that’s honestly where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant amount of weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess it’s been noticeable. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but HR and my sister have both been saying I’ve gotten too thin, which is kind of why I’m here.\n\nFrom my side, I mostly just noticed my suits fitting sharper, you know? But… yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s the same, honestly. I mean, I know people may be seeing me differently than I see myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I kind of focus in on my stomach and my face. And if I think I look soft at all, it’s hard for me not to assume other people will notice it too… like they’ll think I look sloppy or weak. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But it does affect me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, if I look in the mirror and I zero in on my stomach or my face and think I look soft, it kind of changes how I feel about myself. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably isn’t the best way to think about it, but… yeah, it matters to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… probably not always. I think I get kind of fixated when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date.\n\nI’ll zero in on my stomach or my face, and if I think I look a little soft, it feels bigger in my head than it maybe really is. Then I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that may not be completely accurate… but in the moment it feels pretty convincing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Then I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that may not be completely accurate… but in the moment it feels pretty convincing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I see my weight as lower, exactly. I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’d like to think I’m seeing myself realistically, but I know I probably… focus in too much on certain parts.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks for seeing me. Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister have been on me about looking too thin. I don’t really see it the same way, I guess… I mean, my suits fit cleaner, sharper, so it hasn’t felt like a bad thing to me. But my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and people seem concerned, so… I figured I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t want to be dramatic. But yeah, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound up after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I feel this kind of panic. Like I’m losing control, I guess. And then I’m already thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know everyone’s making a pretty big deal about it, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But I’m still jogging, I’m still covering my territory, I’m still hitting my numbers at work, so it doesn’t feel like I’m in some kind of medical emergency or anything. I guess I’d say it’s lower than people are comfortable with, but… I don’t know, I feel like I’m functioning okay.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s the only reason, or like I’m being irrational, but… yeah, a lot of it is that. If I have a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami and the scale is even a pound higher afterward, I get this sort of panic, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already planning how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess gaining weight is a pretty big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is the main thing that drives you to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow or dramatic, but if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already figuring out how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess that fear is a big driver.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat or overweight now, even though other people are telling you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I’m, like, fat exactly… I know that probably wouldn’t make sense to you if everyone’s saying I’m too thin. But when I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I kind of zoom in on my stomach or my face. If I look even a little soft, it really bothers me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult… it’s more like I worry other people are going to see that and think I look sloppy or weak. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d call it normal exactly. Like, I can understand on paper that maybe other people don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut when I’m looking in the mirror before a meeting or a date, I kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look even a little soft, it really bothers me. I start worrying people are going to notice and think I look sloppy, or weak. I know that might sound excessive… but that’s honestly where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant amount of weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess it’s been noticeable. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but HR and my sister have both been saying I’ve gotten too thin, which is kind of why I’m here.\n\nFrom my side, I mostly just noticed my suits fitting sharper, you know? But… yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s the same, honestly. I mean, I know people may be seeing me differently than I see myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I kind of focus in on my stomach and my face. And if I think I look soft at all, it’s hard for me not to assume other people will notice it too… like they’ll think I look sloppy or weak. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But it does affect me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, if I look in the mirror and I zero in on my stomach or my face and think I look soft, it kind of changes how I feel about myself. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I don’t really see it the same way, I guess… I mean, my suits fit cleaner, sharper, so it hasn’t felt like a bad thing to me. But my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and people seem concerned, so… I figured I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t want to be dramatic. But yeah, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound up after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I feel this kind of panic. Like I’m losing control, I guess. And then I’m already thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know everyone’s making a pretty big deal about it, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But I’m still jogging, I’m still covering my territory, I’m still hitting my numbers at work, so it doesn’t feel like I’m in some kind of medical emergency or anything. I guess I’d say it’s lower than people are comfortable with, but… I don’t know, I feel like I’m functioning okay.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s the only reason, or like I’m being irrational, but… yeah, a lot of it is that. If I have a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami and the scale is even a pound higher afterward, I get this sort of panic, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already planning how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess gaining weight is a pretty big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is the main thing that drives you to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow or dramatic, but if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already figuring out how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess that fear is a big driver.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat or overweight now, even though other people are telling you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I’m, like, fat exactly… I know that probably wouldn’t make sense to you if everyone’s saying I’m too thin. But when I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I kind of zoom in on my stomach or my face. If I look even a little soft, it really bothers me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult… it’s more like I worry other people are going to see that and think I look sloppy or weak. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d call it normal exactly. I know that might sound excessive… but that’s honestly where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant amount of weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess it’s been noticeable. I think it’s more about how I think I look… or what the weight means to me, I guess.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks for seeing me. Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister have been on me about looking too thin. I don’t really see it the same way, I guess… I mean, my suits fit cleaner, sharper, so it hasn’t felt like a bad thing to me. But my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and people seem concerned, so… I figured I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t want to be dramatic. But yeah, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound up after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I feel this kind of panic. Like I’m losing control, I guess. And then I’m already thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know everyone’s making a pretty big deal about it, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But I’m still jogging, I’m still covering my territory, I’m still hitting my numbers at work, so it doesn’t feel like I’m in some kind of medical emergency or anything. I guess I’d say it’s lower than people are comfortable with, but… I don’t know, I feel like I’m functioning okay.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s the only reason, or like I’m being irrational, but… yeah, a lot of it is that. If I have a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami and the scale is even a pound higher afterward, I get this sort of panic, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already planning how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess gaining weight is a pretty big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is the main thing that drives you to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow or dramatic, but if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already figuring out how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess that fear is a big driver.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat or overweight now, even though other people are telling you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I’m, like, fat exactly… I know that probably wouldn’t make sense to you if everyone’s saying I’m too thin. But when I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I kind of zoom in on my stomach or my face. If I look even a little soft, it really bothers me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult… it’s more like I worry other people are going to see that and think I look sloppy or weak. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d call it normal exactly. Like, I can understand on paper that maybe other people don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut when I’m looking in the mirror before a meeting or a date, I kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look even a little soft, it really bothers me. I start worrying people are going to notice and think I look sloppy, or weak. I know that might sound excessive… but that’s honestly where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant amount of weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess it’s been noticeable. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but HR and my sister have both been saying I’ve gotten too thin, which is kind of why I’m here.\n\nFrom my side, I mostly just noticed my suits fitting sharper, you know? But… yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s the same, honestly. I mean, I know people may be seeing me differently than I see myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I kind of focus in on my stomach and my face. And if I think I look soft at all, it’s hard for me not to assume other people will notice it too… like they’ll think I look sloppy or weak. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But it does affect me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, if I look in the mirror and I zero in on my stomach or my face and think I look soft, it kind of changes how I feel about myself. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably isn’t the best way to think about it, but… yeah, it matters to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… probably not always. I think I get kind of fixated when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date.\n\nI’ll zero in on my stomach or my face, and if I think I look a little soft, it feels bigger in my head than it maybe really is. Then I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that may not be completely accurate… but in the moment it feels pretty convincing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I see my weight as lower, exactly. It’s more like… when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I focus on certain things—my stomach, my face—and if they look soft to me, that’s all I can really see.\n\nAnd then I start thinking other people are going to notice it too, like they’ll see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably sounds a little harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the same in the mirror as you actually do in real life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’d like to think I’m seeing myself realistically, but I know I probably… focus in too much on certain parts.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft, then that’s all I can see, and it feels like that’s how I really look to everyone else too—like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak.\n\nI know that might not be exactly accurate in real life, but in the moment it’s hard to separate that from what I’m actually seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I’d like to say it’s mostly the mirror, but it’s not just that.\n\nIf I step on the scale and it’s even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this… panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know one pound probably shouldn’t matter that much. I get that. But in the moment it feels like it does, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look or because of how you feel about your health?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how I think I look… or what the weight means to me, I guess.\n\nI don’t usually think of it as a health thing, honestly. Like if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme for just a pound, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks for seeing me. Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister have been on me about looking too thin. I don’t really see it the same way, I guess… I mean, my suits fit cleaner, sharper, so it hasn’t felt like a bad thing to me. But my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and people seem concerned, so… I figured I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t want to be dramatic. But yeah, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound up after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I feel this kind of panic. Like I’m losing control, I guess. And then I’m already thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know everyone’s making a pretty big deal about it, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But I’m still jogging, I’m still covering my territory, I’m still hitting my numbers at work, so it doesn’t feel like I’m in some kind of medical emergency or anything. I guess I’d say it’s lower than people are comfortable with, but… I don’t know, I feel like I’m functioning okay.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s the only reason, or like I’m being irrational, but… yeah, a lot of it is that. If I have a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami and the scale is even a pound higher afterward, I get this sort of panic, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already planning how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess gaining weight is a pretty big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is the main thing that drives you to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow or dramatic, but if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already figuring out how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess that fear is a big driver.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat or overweight now, even though other people are telling you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I’m, like, fat exactly… I know that probably wouldn’t make sense to you if everyone’s saying I’m too thin. But when I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I kind of zoom in on my stomach or my face. If I look even a little soft, it really bothers me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult… it’s more like I worry other people are going to see that and think I look sloppy or weak. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d call it normal exactly. Like, I can understand on paper that maybe other people don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut when I’m looking in the mirror before a meeting or a date, I kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look even a little soft, it really bothers me. I start worrying people are going to notice and think I look sloppy, or weak. I know that might sound excessive… but that’s honestly where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant amount of weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess it’s been noticeable. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but HR and my sister have both been saying I’ve gotten too thin, which is kind of why I’m here.\n\nFrom my side, I mostly just noticed my suits fitting sharper, you know? But… yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s the same, honestly. I mean, I know people may be seeing me differently than I see myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I kind of focus in on my stomach and my face. And if I think I look soft at all, it’s hard for me not to assume other people will notice it too… like they’ll think I look sloppy or weak. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But it does affect me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, if I look in the mirror and I zero in on my stomach or my face and think I look soft, it kind of changes how I feel about myself. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably isn’t the best way to think about it, but… yeah, it matters to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… probably not always. I think I get kind of fixated when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date.\n\nI’ll zero in on my stomach or my face, and if I think I look a little soft, it feels bigger in my head than it maybe really is. Then I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that may not be completely accurate… but in the moment it feels pretty convincing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I see my weight as lower, exactly. I mean, I’d like to say it’s mostly the mirror, but it’s not just that.\n\nIf I step on the scale and it’s even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this… panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know one pound probably shouldn’t matter that much. I get that. But in the moment it feels like it does, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look or because of how you feel about your health?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how I think I look… or what the weight means to me, I guess.\n\nI don’t usually think of it as a health thing, honestly. Like if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme for just a pound, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, if I’m being honest.\n\nEven if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still know what the scale said. And if it was even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I’d get that panic feeling, like I’m losing control. Then I’d start thinking about how to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that sounds maybe more intense than it should be, but… yeah, I think it would still be there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks for seeing me. Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister have been on me about looking too thin. I don’t really see it the same way, I guess… I mean, my suits fit cleaner, sharper, so it hasn’t felt like a bad thing to me. But my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and people seem concerned, so… I figured I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t want to be dramatic. But yeah, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound up after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I feel this kind of panic. Like I’m losing control, I guess. And then I’m already thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know everyone’s making a pretty big deal about it, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But I’m still jogging, I’m still covering my territory, I’m still hitting my numbers at work, so it doesn’t feel like I’m in some kind of medical emergency or anything. I guess I’d say it’s lower than people are comfortable with, but… I don’t know, I feel like I’m functioning okay.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s the only reason, or like I’m being irrational, but… yeah, a lot of it is that. If I have a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami and the scale is even a pound higher afterward, I get this sort of panic, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already planning how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess gaining weight is a pretty big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is the main thing that drives you to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow or dramatic, but if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already figuring out how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess that fear is a big driver.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat or overweight now, even though other people are telling you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I’m, like, fat exactly… I know that probably wouldn’t make sense to you if everyone’s saying I’m too thin. But when I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I kind of zoom in on my stomach or my face. If I look even a little soft, it really bothers me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult… it’s more like I worry other people are going to see that and think I look sloppy or weak. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d call it normal exactly. Like, I can understand on paper that maybe other people don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut when I’m looking in the mirror before a meeting or a date, I kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look even a little soft, it really bothers me. I start worrying people are going to notice and think I look sloppy, or weak. I know that might sound excessive… but that’s honestly where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant amount of weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess it’s been noticeable. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but HR and my sister have both been saying I’ve gotten too thin, which is kind of why I’m here.\n\nFrom my side, I mostly just noticed my suits fitting sharper, you know? But… yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s the same, honestly. I mean, I know people may be seeing me differently than I see myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I kind of focus in on my stomach and my face. And if I think I look soft at all, it’s hard for me not to assume other people will notice it too… like they’ll think I look sloppy or weak. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But it does affect me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, if I look in the mirror and I zero in on my stomach or my face and think I look soft, it kind of changes how I feel about myself. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably isn’t the best way to think about it, but… yeah, it matters to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… probably not always. I think I get kind of fixated when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date.\n\nI’ll zero in on my stomach or my face, and if I think I look a little soft, it feels bigger in my head than it maybe really is. Then I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that may not be completely accurate… but in the moment it feels pretty convincing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I see my weight as lower, exactly. It’s more like… when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I focus on certain things—my stomach, my face—and if they look soft to me, that’s all I can really see.\n\nAnd then I start thinking other people are going to notice it too, like they’ll see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably sounds a little harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the same in the mirror as you actually do in real life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’d like to think I’m seeing myself realistically, but I know I probably… focus in too much on certain parts.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft, then that’s all I can see, and it feels like that’s how I really look to everyone else too—like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak.\n\nI know that might not be exactly accurate in real life, but in the moment it’s hard to separate that from what I’m actually seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know one pound probably shouldn’t matter that much. I get that. Like if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme for just a pound, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, if I’m being honest.\n\nEven if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still know what the scale said. Then I’d start thinking about how to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that sounds maybe more intense than it should be, but… yeah, I think it would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But it does affect me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, if I look in the mirror and I zero in on my stomach or my face and think I look soft, it kind of changes how I feel about myself. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably isn’t the best way to think about it, but… yeah, it matters to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… probably not always. I think I get kind of fixated when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date.\n\nI’ll zero in on my stomach or my face, and if I think I look a little soft, it feels bigger in my head than it maybe really is. Then I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that may not be completely accurate… but in the moment it feels pretty convincing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I see my weight as lower, exactly. It’s more like… when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I focus on certain things—my stomach, my face—and if they look soft to me, that’s all I can really see.\n\nAnd then I start thinking other people are going to notice it too, like they’ll see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably sounds a little harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the same in the mirror as you actually do in real life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’d like to think I’m seeing myself realistically, but I know I probably… focus in too much on certain parts.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft, then that’s all I can see, and it feels like that’s how I really look to everyone else too—like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak.\n\nI know that might not be exactly accurate in real life, but in the moment it’s hard to separate that from what I’m actually seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I’d like to say it’s mostly the mirror, but it’s not just that.\n\nIf I step on the scale and it’s even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this… panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know one pound probably shouldn’t matter that much. I get that. But in the moment it feels like it does, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look or because of how you feel about your health?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how I think I look… or what the weight means to me, I guess.\n\nI don’t usually think of it as a health thing, honestly. Like if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme for just a pound, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, if I’m being honest.\n\nEven if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still know what the scale said. And if it was even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I’d get that panic feeling, like I’m losing control. Then I’d start thinking about how to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that sounds maybe more intense than it should be, but… yeah, I think it would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but before a meeting or a date I’ll look in the mirror and kind of… zero in on my stomach or my face.\n\nIf I think I look soft, I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, I guess. I know that may not be what they’re actually thinking, but it feels that way to me in the moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I guess I’d say it’s lower than people are comfortable with, but… I don’t know, I feel like I’m functioning okay.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s the only reason, or like I’m being irrational, but… yeah, a lot of it is that. If I have a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami and the scale is even a pound higher afterward, I get this sort of panic, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already planning how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess gaining weight is a pretty big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is the main thing that drives you to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow or dramatic, but if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I’m already figuring out how to cut back the next day. So… yeah, I guess that fear is a big driver.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat or overweight now, even though other people are telling you that you're too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I’m, like, fat exactly… I know that probably wouldn’t make sense to you if everyone’s saying I’m too thin. But when I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I kind of zoom in on my stomach or my face. If I look even a little soft, it really bothers me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult… it’s more like I worry other people are going to see that and think I look sloppy or weak. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d call it normal exactly. Like, I can understand on paper that maybe other people don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut when I’m looking in the mirror before a meeting or a date, I kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look even a little soft, it really bothers me. I start worrying people are going to notice and think I look sloppy, or weak. I know that might sound excessive… but that’s honestly where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant amount of weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess it’s been noticeable. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but HR and my sister have both been saying I’ve gotten too thin, which is kind of why I’m here.\n\nFrom my side, I mostly just noticed my suits fitting sharper, you know? But… yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s the same, honestly. I mean, I know people may be seeing me differently than I see myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I kind of focus in on my stomach and my face. And if I think I look soft at all, it’s hard for me not to assume other people will notice it too… like they’ll think I look sloppy or weak. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But it does affect me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, if I look in the mirror and I zero in on my stomach or my face and think I look soft, it kind of changes how I feel about myself. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably isn’t the best way to think about it, but… yeah, it matters to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… probably not always. I think I get kind of fixated when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date.\n\nI’ll zero in on my stomach or my face, and if I think I look a little soft, it feels bigger in my head than it maybe really is. Then I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that may not be completely accurate… but in the moment it feels pretty convincing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I see my weight as lower, exactly. It’s more like… when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I focus on certain things—my stomach, my face—and if they look soft to me, that’s all I can really see.\n\nAnd then I start thinking other people are going to notice it too, like they’ll see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably sounds a little harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the same in the mirror as you actually do in real life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’d like to think I’m seeing myself realistically, but I know I probably… focus in too much on certain parts.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft, then that’s all I can see, and it feels like that’s how I really look to everyone else too—like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak.\n\nI know that might not be exactly accurate in real life, but in the moment it’s hard to separate that from what I’m actually seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I’d like to say it’s mostly the mirror, but it’s not just that.\n\nIf I step on the scale and it’s even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this… panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know one pound probably shouldn’t matter that much. I get that. But in the moment it feels like it does, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look or because of how you feel about your health?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how I think I look… or what the weight means to me, I guess.\n\nI don’t usually think of it as a health thing, honestly. Like if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme for just a pound, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, if I’m being honest.\n\nEven if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still know what the scale said. And if it was even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I’d get that panic feeling, like I’m losing control. Then I’d start thinking about how to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that sounds maybe more intense than it should be, but… yeah, I think it would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but before a meeting or a date I’ll look in the mirror and kind of… zero in on my stomach or my face.\n\nIf I think I look soft, I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, I guess. I know that may not be what they’re actually thinking, but it feels that way to me in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow, but before a meeting or a date I’ll look in the mirror and I’ll focus on my stomach or my face.\n\nIf I think I look… soft, I guess, then I start worrying that people will see me as sloppy or weak. Not necessarily “unattractive” exactly, but something close to that. Like I won’t come across the way I’m supposed to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but HR and my sister have both been saying I’ve gotten too thin, which is kind of why I’m here.\n\nFrom my side, I mostly just noticed my suits fitting sharper, you know? But… yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s the same, honestly. I mean, I know people may be seeing me differently than I see myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I kind of focus in on my stomach and my face. And if I think I look soft at all, it’s hard for me not to assume other people will notice it too… like they’ll think I look sloppy or weak. I know that might not be totally reasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, shallow or extreme. But it does affect me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, if I look in the mirror and I zero in on my stomach or my face and think I look soft, it kind of changes how I feel about myself. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably isn’t the best way to think about it, but… yeah, it matters to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… probably not always. I think I get kind of fixated when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date.\n\nI’ll zero in on my stomach or my face, and if I think I look a little soft, it feels bigger in my head than it maybe really is. Then I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that may not be completely accurate… but in the moment it feels pretty convincing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I see my weight as lower, exactly. It’s more like… when I look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, I focus on certain things—my stomach, my face—and if they look soft to me, that’s all I can really see.\n\nAnd then I start thinking other people are going to notice it too, like they’ll see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably sounds a little harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the same in the mirror as you actually do in real life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’d like to think I’m seeing myself realistically, but I know I probably… focus in too much on certain parts.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and kind of lock onto my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft, then that’s all I can see, and it feels like that’s how I really look to everyone else too—like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak.\n\nI know that might not be exactly accurate in real life, but in the moment it’s hard to separate that from what I’m actually seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I’d like to say it’s mostly the mirror, but it’s not just that.\n\nIf I step on the scale and it’s even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this… panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know one pound probably shouldn’t matter that much. I get that. But in the moment it feels like it does, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look or because of how you feel about your health?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how I think I look… or what the weight means to me, I guess.\n\nI don’t usually think of it as a health thing, honestly. Like if the scale is even a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this panic feeling, like I’m losing control. And then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme for just a pound, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, if I’m being honest.\n\nEven if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still know what the scale said. And if it was even like a pound higher after a client dinner or a weekend in Miami, I’d get that panic feeling, like I’m losing control. Then I’d start thinking about how to cut back the next day.\n\nI know that sounds maybe more intense than it should be, but… yeah, I think it would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but before a meeting or a date I’ll look in the mirror and kind of… zero in on my stomach or my face.\n\nIf I think I look soft, I start worrying people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, I guess. I know that may not be what they’re actually thinking, but it feels that way to me in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it. I don’t love saying it that way, because it sounds kind of shallow, but before a meeting or a date I’ll look in the mirror and I’ll focus on my stomach or my face.\n\nIf I think I look… soft, I guess, then I start worrying that people will see me as sloppy or weak. Not necessarily “unattractive” exactly, but something close to that. Like I won’t come across the way I’m supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you have a distorted view of what you look like?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it “distorted,” exactly, because that sounds like I’m completely seeing something that isn’t there. But I do think I get really focused on certain things.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and kind of zero in on my stomach or my face. And if I think I look soft, I immediately worry people are going to see me as sloppy or weak.\n\nI know that might not be totally rational, but in the moment it feels pretty convincing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you see yourself as heavier than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly like I think I’m much heavier than I am. It’s more that I’ll look in the mirror, especially before a meeting or a date, and I’ll zero in on my stomach or my face.\n\nIf I think I look a little soft, then my mind goes straight to, like… people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where it goes for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
